       82 +// eligibleCapabilitiesFromDescription parses the pipeline too
          +l's "Eligible                                                 
       83 +// capabilities (...): a, b, c." line out of its description i
          +nto a set, so                                                 
       84 +// tests can check exact membership rather than substring-matc
          +hing the whole                                                
       85 +// description (which would be fooled by, e.g., "find" appeari
          +ng inside                                                     
       86 +// "findings").                                               
       87 +func eligibleCapabilitiesFromDescription(t *testing.T, desc st
          +ring) map[string]bool {                                       
       88 +  t.Helper()                                                  
       89 +  const marker = "may appear as a stage): "                   
       90 +  idx := strings.Index(desc, marker)                          
       91 +  if idx < 0 {                                                
       92 +    t.Fatalf("pipeline description missing the eligible-capabi
          +lities line: %s", desc)                                       
       93 +  }                                                           
       94 +  list := desc[idx+len(marker):]                              
       95 +  if end := strings.Index(list, ".\n"); end >= 0 {            
       96 +    list = list[:end]                                         
       97 +  }                                                           
       98 +  set := make(map[string]bool)                                
       99 +  for _, name := range strings.Split(list, ", ") {            
      100 +    set[strings.TrimSuffix(name, ".")] = true                 
      101 +  }                                                           
      102 +  return set                                                  
      103 +}                                                             
      104 +

  ⎿  Added 70 lines, removed 6 lines
      195    generic mapping can't express (e.g. `find` needs its search 
           root *first* and its
      196    name filters combined into one parenthesized OR group).
      197  - **Builtin** (`pwd`, `largest_files`) — answered in Go with n
           o subprocess at all,
      198 -  for questions a single command can't answer. `largest_files`
          - is the clearest                                              
      199 -  example: "biggest files" is a `du -a | sort -rn | head` *pip
          -eline*, which the                                             
      200 -  no-shell rule forbids — so the builtin walks the tree once,

      201 -  in a bounded heap, and returns just those ranked lines. Outp

      202 -  construction and never floods the model's context.

          +rgest_files` is                                               
      199 +  the clearest example: "biggest files" is a `du -a | sort -rn
          + | head` *pipeline*                                           
      200 +  idiom — a literal shell pipe is still forbidden, and even th
          +e server-side                                                 
      201 +  `pipeline` tool below would need the model to assemble three
          + stages for                                                   
      202 +  something this common — so the builtin walks the tree once, 
          +keeps only the                                                
      203 +  top N in a bounded heap, and returns just those ranked lines
          + in a single call.                                            
      204 +  Output is small by construction and never floods the model's
          + context.                                                     
      205  
      204 -A mutating capability is fulfilled differently — see the next 
          -section.                                                      
      206 +A mutating capability is fulfilled differently — see [Mutating
      207 +operations](#mutating-operations-stage--execute--undo) below. 
          +Composing                                                     
      208 +*multiple* read-only capabilities together (when no single one
          + or named                                                     
      209 +builtin already answers the question) is the `pipeline` tool's
          + job — see the                                                
      210 +next section.                                                 
      211  
      212  ---
      213  
      214 +## `pipeline`: composing capabilities                         
      215 +                                                              
      216 +Some questions need more than one capability chained together 
          +— "how many                                                   
      217 +`.conf` files are under `/etc`?" needs `find` to list matches 
          +and `wc` to count                                             
      218 +them — but don't justify a bespoke builtin the way `largest_fi
          +les` did for                                                  
      219 +"biggest files." The `pipeline` tool is the general escape hat
          +ch: an ordered                                                
      220 +list of stages where each stage's raw output feeds the next st
          +age's input,                                                  
      221 +the server-side equivalent of a unix `a | b | c` pipe with no 
          +shell involved                                                
      222 +(every stage is still launched via `exec.CommandContext` with 
          +its own                                                       
      223 +validated, explicit argv — see [Why this server is safe to    
      224 +expose](#why-this-server-is-safe-to-expose)).                 
      225 +                                                              
      226 +```                                                           
      227 +pipeline(stages: [                                            
      228 +  { capability: "find", params: { path: "/etc", extensions: ["
          +conf"] } },                                                   
      229 +  { capability: "wc",   params: { lines: true } }             
      230 +])                                                            
      231 +```                                                           
      232 +                                                              
      233 +Notice the second stage omits `paths` — that's how a stage con
          +sumes the                                                     
      234 +*previous* stage's output instead of a named file, the same wa
          +y `wc` reads                                                  
      235 +stdin in a real shell pipe when given no file argument.       
      236 +                                                              
      237 +**Scope, deliberately narrow:**                               
      238 +- **Read-only, binary-backed capabilities only.** Builtins (`p
          +wd`,                                                          
      239 +  `largest_files`) have no subprocess to pipe; mutators (`mkdi
          +r`,                                                           
      240 +  `write_setting`) don't run in one step at all. This also mea
          +ns a pipeline                                                 
      241 +  never mutates anything — there is nothing to roll back, ever
          +, so composition                                              
      242 +  stays orthogonal to the mutation phase's transactional machi
          +nery.                                                         
      243 +- **`wc`, `grep`, `sort`, and `head`** can appear as a *non-fi
          +rst* stage,                                                   
      244 +  reading the prior stage's output from stdin instead of a fil
          +e (the classic                                                
      245 +  unix filter idiom). A capability that accepts this is marked
      246 +  `accepts_stdin` in its manifest entry; calling one of these 
          +four directly                                                 
      247 +  (outside a pipeline) without a file argument is refused imme
          +diately with a                                                
      248 +  clear error rather than hanging forever waiting for input th
          +at will never                                                 
      249 +  arrive.                                                     
      250 +- **Limits**: at most 5 stages; each intermediate stage's raw 
          +output is capped                                              
      251 +  at 1 MiB (generous on purpose — that data never reaches the 
          +model, it only                                                
      252 +  has to fit in the server's own memory; the *final* stage's o
          +utput still goes                                              
      253 +  through the normal 8 KB model-facing compaction). A failing 
          +stage (non-zero                                               
      254 +  exit, or exceeding that cap) aborts the whole pipeline immed
          +iately, naming                                                
      255 +  which stage and why.                                        
      256 +- **Sequential, not concurrently streamed.** Each stage runs t
          +o completion and                                              
      257 +  its captured output becomes the next stage's input, rather t
          +han every stage                                               
      258 +  running concurrently with a live OS-level pipe between them.
          + Simpler and                                                  
      259 +  exactly as correct at this project's scale (a handful of cap
          +ped stages); see                                              
      260 +  `docs/issues/note-pipeline-design-decisions.md` for the reas
          +oning.                                                        
      261 +                                                              
      262 +**Nudged toward named operations first.** The tool's own descr
          +iption tells                                                  
      263 +the model to check the relevant domain tool's operation menu a
          +nd prefer a                                                   
      264 +single named operation whenever one exists — composing a pipel
          +ine is meant for                                              
      265 +the long tail no named capability covers yet. This is checked 
          +mechanically,                                                 
      266 +not just hoped for: several eval cases assert `forbid_tools: [
          +"pipeline"]`                                                  
      267 +for prompts a single named operation already answers (see     
      268 +[Evals](#evals)).                                             
      269 +                                                              
      270 +---                                                           
      271 +

      273  
      274  Read-only capabilities are a pure function of their parameters
           , so they run and

⏺ The pipeline tool is implemented, tested, documented, and committed. Summary:

  What landed:
  - A new pipeline MCP tool composing read-only capabilities, with each stage's
  raw stdout piped into the next stage's stdin — the server-side a | b | c
  idiom, no shell, no model-mediated data passing.
  - Two new capabilities (sort, head) plus wc/grep relaxed to accept piped
  input, all gated by a new AcceptsStdin registry field with a boot-time
  precondition check and a runtime guard that refuses (rather than hangs on) a
  standalone call with no input source.
  - Scoped to read-only, binary-backed stages only — builtins and mutators
  excluded, so nothing ever needs rollback.
  - Limits: 5 stages max, 1 MiB intermediate cap, sequential/buffered execution
  (not true concurrent OS-level streaming — documented why).
  - The tool's description is generated live from the registry, so its "eligible
  capabilities" list can never drift from what it'll actually accept, and
  explicitly nudges the model toward named operations first.
  - Full test coverage: a real find | wc round trip at both the engine and
  protocol level, max-stage/size-cap enforcement, rejection of builtin/mutator
  stages, and the standalone hang-prevention guard.
  - Eval coverage: a new case proving the model reaches for pipeline when
  warranted, plus forbid_tools: ["pipeline"] added to existing single-op cases
  to mechanically catch over-use.

  Build, vet, gofmt, and the full test suite (including -race) are clean. I have
  not run a live eval pass — let me know if you want me to (same cost caveat as
  before).

❯ Consider the following Copilot comments on the PR, and address if             
  appropriate:                                                                  
  https://github.com/kinnoo-project/mcp-server-mac-os/pull/5                    
                                                                                
  (internal/engine/pipeline.go) maxPipelineStageBytes is documented as an       
  intermediate-stage cap, but the current check applies to every stage          
  (including the final stage, and even a 1-stage pipeline). This makes          
  pipeline([find...]) fail on large outputs even though standalone Run would    
  just compact the result, and it contradicts the comments/README describing    
  the cap as intermediate-only.                                                 
                                                                                
  (internal/registry/manifests/filesystem.json) grep now allows omitting paths  
  (to read piped stdin), but paths is still marked with arg.kind = "none". The  
  standalone-call guard (missingPositionalInput) only checks positional args,   
  so calling filesystem.grep directly with just pattern will run against empty  
  stdin (Go wires nil stdin to /dev/null) instead of being refused as described 
  in the README/manifest text. Mark paths as positional so standalone calls     
  without paths are rejected, while non-first pipeline stages can still omit    
  it.                                                                           
                                                                                
  (internal/server/pipeline.go) RunPipeline already prefixes its errors with    
  pipeline:. Wrapping it again with errorResult("pipeline: %v", err) duplicates 
  the prefix (e.g. pipeline: pipeline: stage 2 ...), making errors noisier and  
  harder to read.                                                               
                                                                                
  Also, since we are doing this PR review often, create a Claude skill          
  '/pr-review' that either reviews a PR (if it hasn't been reviewed by another  
  agent such as Copilot) or addresses PR comments from another agent, depending 
  on the user prompt. The user should specify in the prompt either a URL to     
  the PR or a PR#. Save your review or your fixes based on PR comments (use the 
  format `(code-location) comment` I have above for documenting the PR          
  comments) in docs/pr-reviews/pr#.md where # is the pr number. Does this make  
  sense?

⏺ Done. Summary:

  PR #5 Copilot comments — all addressed and pushed (913da88):
  1. The 1 MiB intermediate-stage cap was wrongly applying to the final stage
  too — fixed to only check non-final stages, with tests split to cover both
  cases.
  2. grep's paths param was tagged arg.kind: "none", which meant the
  standalone-stdin-hang guard could never find it — a bare grep(pattern: ...)
  call would silently run against empty stdin instead of being refused. Fixed by
  tagging it positional (a structural marker only — buildGrep still ignores Arg
  for actual argv assembly) and added a regression test.
  3. Double pipeline: pipeline: ... error prefix — fixed.

  All three replied to inline on the PR, full suite (including -race) green.

  New /pr-review skill at .claude/skills/pr-review.md, per your two decisions:
  review-mode never posts to GitHub (local docs/pr-reviews/pr{N}.md only), and
  it's project-scoped here alongside the existing verify-pipeline.md. It
  codifies exactly the workflow we've now run twice: detect fix-vs-review mode
  from PR state or explicit instruction, triage each comment in context,
  fix-or-explain, verify, commit/push, reply on GitHub, and record everything
  locally.

  Also retroactively wrote docs/pr-reviews/pr4.md and pr5.md capturing the
  comments already resolved this session, in the skill's format — so the
  historical record exists even though those fixes predated the skill.

  Your design makes sense as stated — the one thing I locked down explicitly
  rather than guessing was whether review-mode should post to GitHub, since
  that's a "visible to others" action worth deciding once rather than
  re-confirming every invocation.

⏺ Both osascript and mdfind already live in the trusted binary directories — so
  this is structurally feasible with zero new dependencies. Let me walk through
  what I know and then nail down the key design forks with you before writing
  the plan.

  Feasibility verdict: yes, both are possible, no third-party library needed

  Sending/composing mail — macOS ships Mail.app's own AppleScript dictionary
  (tell application "Mail" to make new outgoing message...). This is invoked via
  osascript, a real binary already in our trusted dirs — no shell, no new
  dependency, fits the existing exec.CommandContext model exactly. The one new
  wrinkle: AppleScript source is a more expressive language than a CLI flag set,
  so naively string-concatenating a subject/body into a script would be an
  AppleScript-injection bug, the direct analogue of shell injection. The fix is
  the same principle CLAUDE.md already mandates for shells: never splice
  untrusted data into the executed code — ship a fixed, embedded .applescript 
  template (like our JSON manifests, embedded into the binary) that takes its
  parameters via on run argv, so untrusted strings arrive as data, never as
  script text.

  Searching mail — two real options: AppleScript whose-clause search within
  Mail.app, or mdfind (Spotlight) querying indexed mail content directly
  (kind:email) with zero Automation-permission friction. mdfind is the simpler,
  lower-risk path — it's structurally identical to our existing find/grep
  capabilities (a trusted binary, explicit argv, read-only, no AppleScript
  surface at all).

  The one real design problem: sending mail is irreversible. There's no
  "unsend." The project's own roadmap already flags irreversible operations as
  unbuilt infrastructure ("a heavier confirmation gate plus a Trash/staging 
  fallback... instead of a false promise of undo"). A draft, by contrast, is
  genuinely reversible (delete the draft = undo) and fits the existing stage → 
  execute → undo machinery cleanly.

  One-time friction worth knowing about: the first Mail.app automation call
  triggers a macOS Automation permission prompt for the host process — the same
  UX pattern already documented in the README for Full-Disk-Access, not a new
  category of friction.

  Before I write the plan, three real forks:

❯ Why would Applescript (direct Mail.app) be more precise than Spotlight /      
  mdfind? What are some examples where this would be the case. Maybe for new    
  mail? Or very old mail?                                                       

⏺ Good question — let me be precise about what "precise" actually means here,
  since it's really two different things: scoping accuracy and
  coverage/freshness.

  Coverage/freshness gaps (mdfind's weak spot)

  Spotlight indexes mail asynchronously, in the background, separately from
  Mail.app's own live data. That creates real gaps:

  - Brand-new mail: a message that just arrived may not be in the Spotlight
  index yet — there's an indexing lag, especially under load (e.g., a big
  attachment, many simultaneous emails arriving, or the indexer busy with other
  work). AppleScript talking to Mail.app directly sees its live in-memory state,
  so a just-arrived message shows up immediately with no lag.
  - Very old mail, especially after a Mac migration: if a mailbox was just
  imported/re-downloaded (new Mac, re-added account, restored from backup),
  Spotlight has to re-index the whole backlog from scratch, which can take a
  long time for a large archive. Until that finishes, old mail is effectively
  invisible to mdfind even though it's sitting right there in Mail.app.
  - Junk/Spam folders specifically: these are commonly excluded from Spotlight's
  index by design (privacy/spam-isolation reasoning), so mdfind may return
  nothing for a message you can see plainly in Mail.app's Junk mailbox.

  Scoping precision (AppleScript's strength)

  - Account/mailbox-exact queries: "search only my Work account's Inbox" is
  something AppleScript expresses natively by name (mailbox "Inbox" of account 
  "Work"). mdfind can be constrained with -onlyin <path>, but Mail's on-disk
  layout uses opaque account UUIDs as directory names — there's no clean way to
  say "Work account" to mdfind without first resolving that name to a filesystem
  path some other way.
  - Live state, not indexed content: "unread" and "flagged" are mutable UI
  states, not necessarily reliable Spotlight metadata. AppleScript reads
  Mail.app's actual current state (whose read status is false); Spotlight's
  snapshot may lag or not track these consistently.

  The honest tradeoff going the other way

  AppleScript's whose-clause search evaluates live data by walking the mailbox,
  which is slow on a large mailbox (thousands of messages) — Spotlight's
  precomputed index is much faster for "search everything" style queries. So
  it's not a strict win either way: mdfind is faster and broader; AppleScript is
  more current and more precisely scoped, but slower on big mailboxes.

  Given that, the practical split would be: mdfind for general "find an email
  mentioning X" questions (probably the common case), and reserve
  AppleScript-based search only if you specifically want account/mailbox-scoped
  or recency-sensitive queries. Want me to fold both in (a scope parameter
  choosing implementation under the hood), or pick one for v1 and keep the other
  as a documented future option?

  feature upgrade for cases that need more precision (checking for new mail,    
  checking for archived or spam  mail, etc...) - document docs/issues/          

⏺ Two important implementation realities surfaced while thinking this through,
  plus the category-name decision — let me fold them in and write the full plan.

  Realization 1 — "irreversible, heavily gated" already fits the existing 
  architecture with no new infrastructure. registry.Irreversible and
  registry.RiskHigh are already defined enum values, just never used yet.
  StagedPlan.Inverse = nil already means "no undo offered," and server.Execute
  already special-cases that ("Applied X. This change cannot be undone."). So
  send_mail just becomes the first real user of machinery that already exists —
  no new engine/server code needed, just a new mutator.

  Realization 2 — mdfind alone only returns file paths, not subjects/senders. To
  get something useful, search_mail needs to run mdfind then mdls per result to
  pull out subject/sender/date — meaning it has to be a builtin (in-process Go
  composing two subprocess calls), exactly like largest_files, not a simple
  one-shot capability.

  I'll decide the category name myself (mail, matching the
  filesystem/preferences precedent) since it's a cheap, reversible choice.
  Writing the full plan now.

❯ I'm wondering if we can call the category name something like                 
  application/mail, application.mail, application-mail, or even app-mail - so   
  it's clear that it comprises a broader category + a subcategory. Does this    
  make sense?                                                                   

⏺ Good instinct, but there's a real constraint worth checking before picking the
  separator. Let me verify what characters are actually safe in a tool name.

  Searched for 3 patterns (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Found the constraint: the MCP SDK's own validator (validToolNameRune in
  tool.go) only allows letters, digits, _, -, and . — so / is rejected outright
  (confirmed by the SDK's own test cases). A . would pass the SDK, but
  Anthropic's Messages API tool-name validation is stricter
  (alphanumeric/underscore/hyphen only, no dots) — and since our eval harness
  forwards tool definitions straight to that API, a dot risks being rejected one
  layer up even though the SDK itself would accept it. That rules out
  application/mail and application.mail; application-mail or app-mail
  (hyphenated) are the safe versions of your idea.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Given / and . are unsafe for tool names (SDK rejects /, Anthropic's API 
     likely rejects . too), which naming should the mail category use? → 
     application-mail (Recommended)

     Plan: Mail capabilities — search_mail (Spotlight) + send_mail (AppleScript,
     irreversible)

     Context

     Brainstormed whether the server could usefully automate Mail.app. Verdict:
     yes, with zero new dependencies — osascript (AppleScript automation) and
     mdfind/mdls (Spotlight) are both already-trusted binaries under
     /usr/bin, confirmed present on this machine. Two capabilities, in a new
     application-mail category:

     - search_mail (read-only) — Spotlight-backed search.
     - send_mail (mutating, irreversible) — composes and actually sends
     a real email via Mail.app's AppleScript dictionary. There is no "unsend,"
     so this is deliberately gated as irreversible/high-risk rather than given
     a false undo promise.

     Decided in discussion:
     - search_mail uses mdfind/Spotlight for v1, not AppleScript's whose-clause
     search. Spotlight is faster and has no Automation-permission friction, but
     has real gaps (indexing lag on brand-new mail, slow/incomplete re-indexing
     of huge archives after a migration, Junk/Spam often excluded by design,
     imprecise account/mailbox scoping via opaque UUID paths). AppleScript
     search
     is more precise (live Mail.app state, exact account/mailbox scoping,
     accurate read/flagged status) but slower on large mailboxes and needs the
     Automation permission. Document AppleScript search as a deferred
     precision upgrade in docs/issues/, not build it now.
     - send_mail is allowed to really send in v1 (not draft-only), gated by the
     existing irreversible/risk: high machinery rather than new
     infrastructure (see Realization 1 above) — risk and irreversibility are
     shown plainly in the staged preview, and the existing execute confirmation
     gate (client approval prompt) is the enforcement point, same as every other
     mutator.
     - Category name: application-mail (new domain tool, same pattern as
     filesystem/preferences).

     Design

     AppleScript safety pattern (the injection-equivalent of the no-shell axiom)

     AppleScript source is a more expressive language than a flag set, so never
     string-concatenate untrusted data into script source. Pattern: a fixed Go
     string constant holding on run argv ... end run AppleScript, invoked as
     osascript -e <fixed source> with to/subject/body appended as
     additional argv elements — they bind to AppleScript's argv parameter as
     plain string data, never parsed as code. This mirrors the project's
     existing
     positional-argv-only discipline (.claude/rules/darwin-execution.md).

     application-mail category — internal/registry/manifests/mail.json (NEW)

     [
       { "name": "search_mail", "category": "application-mail", "binary": "",
     "reversibility": "read_only",
         "risk": "low", "builder": "search_mail",
         "params": [
           { "name": "query", "type": "string", "required": true, "arg":
     {"kind":"none"},
             "description": "Free-text search across mail 
     subjects/senders/content via Spotlight." },
           { "name": "limit", "type": "int", "required": false, "default": 20,
     "arg": {"kind":"none"},
             "description": "Maximum number of results (default 20, capped at 
     50)." }
         ] },
       { "name": "send_mail", "category": "application-mail", "binary":
     "osascript", "reversibility": "irreversible",
         "risk": "high", "builder": "send_mail",
         "params": [
           { "name": "to", "type": "string_list", "required": true, "arg":
     {"kind":"none"},
             "description": "Recipient email address(es)." },
           { "name": "subject", "type": "string", "required": true, "arg":
     {"kind":"none"} },
           { "name": "body", "type": "string", "required": true, "arg":
     {"kind":"none"} }
         ] }
     ]

     search_mail — internal/engine/builtins_mail.go (NEW)

     A builtin (like largest_files): runs mdfind -onlyin ~/Library/Mail 
     "<query>"
     (scope by directory rather than a kind:email keyword — simpler and avoids
     building a compound boolean query string from untrusted input), caps
     results
     to limit (≤50), then runs mdls -name kMDItemSubject -name 
     kMDItemDisplayName -name kMDItemContentCreationDate <path> per result
     (bounded by the same
     limit) to render a clean ranked list — composing two trusted-binary calls
     in
     Go, never a shell pipe between them.

     send_mail — internal/engine/mutate_mail.go (NEW)

     A mutator (stageSendMail), registered in mutate.go's mutators map.
     Light validation only (non-empty recipients look like addresses — catch
     obvious mistakes before showing a preview, not full RFC822 validation).
     StagedPlan:
     - Forward: Command{Binary: "osascript", Args: [-e, <fixed AppleScript 
     source>, to..., subject, body]} — the script composes and immediately
     sends via tell application "Mail".
     - Inverse: nil (irreversible — server.Execute's existing nil-inverse
     branch already renders "this change cannot be undone").
     - Preview: shows the verbatim recipient(s)/subject/body (not
     summarized — the human approving this must see exactly what will be sent)
     plus an explicit "this will send a real email and cannot be undone" line.

     No changes needed to internal/policy (osascript/mdfind/mdls already under
     /usr/bin), engine.go's ValidateBuilders/Run, or server/tools.go's
     mutation plumbing — everything routes through the existing builder/builtin/
     mutator dispatch and the existing stage→execute→undo machinery. This is a
     good validation that the architecture generalizes.

     docs/issues/note-applescript-mail-search-deferred.md (NEW)

     Records the AppleScript-vs-Spotlight precision tradeoff from this session
     (indexing lag on new mail, slow re-indexing of old/migrated archives,
     Junk/Spam exclusion, imprecise UUID-path account scoping vs. AppleScript's
     exact name-based scoping and live read/flagged state) as a documented
     future
     upgrade path, per the discussion — not built now.

     Eval cases (evals/cases/mail.json, NEW)

     - A search_mail case is safe to actually execute (read-only) — but its real
     results (subjects/senders) would flow through the Anthropic API as part of
     the tool_result. Pick a query guaranteed not to match real mail (e.g. a
     random token) so the case proves correct tool selection without ever
     sending real personal email content to the API.
     - send_mail must be stage-only, single-turn, forbid_tools: ["execute"] —
     unlike mkdir/write_setting, there is no self-cleaning undo turn possible
     here (nothing to undo). A live eval run must never reach execute for this
     capability, full stop.

     Critical files

     - NEW internal/registry/manifests/mail.json
     - NEW internal/engine/builtins_mail.go (+ test) — search_mail
     - NEW internal/engine/mutate_mail.go (+ test) — send_mail
     - NEW docs/issues/note-applescript-mail-search-deferred.md
     - NEW evals/cases/mail.json
     - internal/server/integration_test.go — tool surface now expects an
     application-mail domain tool too
     - README.md / docs/TESTS.md updates per CLAUDE.md §8

     Verification

     - go build ./... && go vet ./... && gofmt -l . && go test ./...
     - Manual, opt-in only: a real osascript Mail.app automation call will

⏺ Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 12 lines, removed 1 line
      422  - **macOS permission model.** The server runs as the user that
            started it and
      423    inherits their Full-Disk-Access, Files-and-Folders, and POSI
           X permissions.
      424    Permission denials surface verbatim from the underlying util
           ity (look for
      425 -  `[stderr]` in tool output).                                 
      425 +  `[stderr]` in tool output). Automating Mail.app (`send_mail`
          +) additionally                                                
      426 +  requires a one-time Automation permission grant, prompted th
          +e first time                                                  
      427 +  it runs — same UX pattern as Full-Disk-Access, just a differ
          +ent macOS                                                     
      428 +  permission category (Apple Events rather than file access). 
      429 +- **No AppleScript injection.** `send_mail` is the one capabil
          +ity that drives                                               
      430 +  a scripting language richer than a flag set, so the no-shell
          + discipline                                                   
      431 +  above gets a named counterpart for it: the AppleScript sourc
          +e                                                             
      432 +  (`internal/engine/mutate_mail.go`'s `sendMailAppleScript`) i
          +s a fixed,                                                    
      433 +  reviewed constant, never built by concatenating model-suppli
          +ed text.                                                      
      434 +  Recipients/subject/body arrive as plain `argv` elements boun
          +d by                                                          
      435 +  AppleScript's own `on run argv` handler — data, never parsed
          + as code,                                                     
      436 +  exactly like every other capability's positional arguments. 
      437

⏺ All tasks complete. Summary:

  What landed:
  - New application-mail domain tool with two capabilities:
    - search_mail (read-only) — a builtin composing mdfind (find matches) + mdls
  (extract subject/sender/date per result), since Spotlight alone only returns
  file paths.
    - send_mail (the registry's first irreversible mutator) — composes and sends
  a real email via a fixed AppleScript template invoked through osascript, with
  recipients/subject/body passed as plain argv data (never spliced into script
  source — the AppleScript-injection-equivalent of the no-shell axiom).
  - Confirmed the existing Inverse = nil / execute's nil-inverse branch needed
  zero new engine or server infrastructure — send_mail is just the first real
  user of machinery that was already there.
  - Documented the Spotlight-vs-AppleScript precision tradeoff as a deferred
  future option in docs/issues/note-applescript-mail-search-deferred.md, per
  your request.
  - Full test coverage at every layer, with two hard safety invariants enforced
  throughout: no test ever executes send_mail's real Forward command (would send
  a real, unrecoverable email), and search_mail tests use a query engineered to
  match nothing (no real personal email content ever touches the test suite or
  the eval harness's Anthropic API calls).
  - Eval case for send_mail is single-turn with forbid_tools: ["execute"] and is
  never followed by a confirmation — a live eval run can structurally never
  reach execute for it.

  Build, vet, gofmt, and the full test suite (including -race) are all green,
  and I verified Mail.app's running process predates this session (never
  launched or automated by any test).

✻ Cooked for 14m 22s

※ recap: We added a Mail domain (search_mail and irreversible send_mail) to the macOS MCP server and just verified everything builds, tests, and committed cleanly. Next: decide whether to run a live eval check or move on to another capability.

❯ Simple email works great! Can the email capability handle attachments? Can I  
  forward an email? And how do 'replies' work? And what about line breaks and   
  paragraphs in the email body - how would I do that?                           

⏺ Good questions — let me give you the honest current state, then what each
  would actually take.

  Current state: none of these exist yet — only a brand-new compose-and-send

  send_mail today takes exactly to / subject / body and creates a fresh outgoing
  message. No attachments, no forwarding, no replying.

  Line breaks / paragraphs — already works, nothing to build

  Just include real newline characters in the body string, e.g.:
  "Hi Alice,\n\nThanks for the update — sounds good.\n\nBest,\nJerry"
  Mail's content property is plain text, and it renders embedded line breaks
  as-is — a blank line between \n\n becomes a paragraph break. This isn't a code
  change; it's just how you pass the parameter. (I haven't sent a live test to
  visually confirm the rendering, since that would be a real, unrecoverable send
  — happy to do a real one if you want to actually see it.)

  Attachments — feasible, needs new work

  Mail's AppleScript dictionary supports attachments (make new attachment with 
  properties {file name: ...}). The implementation wrinkle: today's argv layout
  is [subject, body, recipient1, recipient2, ...] — a flat list with no
  delimiter. Adding a second variable-length list (attachment paths) cleanly
  needs a small layout change, e.g. [subject, body, recipientCount, recipient1, 
  ..., attachment1, ...]. Also needs read-only validation at staging time (the
  file must actually exist — same spirit as mkdir's existing-path check) and the
  preview should list attached filenames so a human approving execute knows
  what's going out, not just the body text.

  Forward / reply — feasible, but needs a missing piece first

  Mail's AppleScript has forward and reply commands that take an existing 
  message reference. The catch: search_mail currently returns Spotlight file
  paths (.emlx files), which is a different identifier space than what
  Mail.app's own object model uses to locate a message (it wants a message id,
  roughly the RFC822 Message-ID header). So forward/reply needs search_mail
  extended to also surface that stable ID, plus a new mutator (forward_mail /
  reply_mail) that locates the message by it, then forwards/replies with your
  new content. This is a bigger, two-part addition, not a quick extension of
  send_mail.

  ---
  Want me to scope and build attachments first (smaller, self-contained), or
  both attachments + reply/forward together? I'd plan it the same way as the
  mail work before — happy to do that now if you want to proceed.

✻ Crunched for 1m 11s

  either indicate the specific path to the file(s) to be attached (preferred)   
  or a description of where the file is (e.g., "in the current directory", "in  
  a subfolder called 'scratch' within the Downloads folder", etc). As a stretch 
  goal (or maybe we do this later), we should allow a prompt such as "Find my   
  2025 federal tax return on my laptop, and send it to <email-address>..."      
  which would invoke the pipeline tool and execute a "find" followed by "send   
  email".                                                                       
                                                                                
  During the staging phase and before execution (i.e., sending the email), we   
  should find a way to present a 'preview' of the email that will be sent, if   
  possible. Something like:                                                     
                                                                                
  The following email will be sent to <email-addresses>:                        
  Subject: ride from airport                                                    
  Body:                                                                         
  Can you pick me up at 5:00pm from the airport? I'm flying Delta.              
  Thanks!                                                                       
  -Jerry                                                                        
                                                                                
  Send this email?                                                              

⏺ Good scope, and it actually simplifies the stretch goal: pipeline can't
  include send_mail anyway (mutators are deliberately excluded — nothing to roll
  back), so "find my tax return and email it" doesn't need pipeline changes at
  all — it's just two ordinary sequential tool calls (find, then send_mail with
  the resulting path). Once attachments exist, that scenario already works. Let
  me implement attachments + the restyled preview.

  One thing I want to flag honestly upfront: I can't verify the exact osascript
  attachment syntax without actually automating your real Mail.app (which needs
  the one-time permission grant and would be a real side effect
  mid-implementation). I'll implement it against the well-documented standard
  idiom, but you should do the first live test rather than assume it's proven.

      39  // sendMailAppleScript composes and immediately sends a message
          . Argv layout
      37 -// (fixed, not model-controlled): item 1 = subject, item 2 = bo
         -dy, items 3..N                                                 
      38 -// = one or more recipient addresses — chosen so the two fixed-
         -position                                                       
      39 -// scalars come first and the variable-length recipient list tr
         -ails, with no                                                  
      40 -// delimiter needed.                                           
      40 +// (fixed, not model-controlled): item 1 = subject, item 2 = bo
         +dy, item 3 =                                                   
      41 +// the recipient count N (as a string), items 4..3+N = recipien
         +t addresses,                                                   
      42 +// and any remaining items = attachment paths. The explicit cou
         +nt is what                                                     
      43 +// lets two variable-length lists (recipients, attachments) sha
         +re one flat                                                    
      44 +// argv unambiguously, without inventing a delimiter character 
         +that some                                                      
      45 +// recipient or path might legitimately contain.               
      46  const sendMailAppleScript = `on run argv
      47    set theSubject to item 1 of argv
      48    set theBody to item 2 of argv
      44 -  set recipientList to items 3 thru (count of argv) of argv    
      49 +  set recipientCount to (item 3 of argv) as integer            
      50 +  set recipientList to {}                                      
      51 +  if recipientCount > 0 then set recipientList to items 4 thru 
         +(3 + recipientCount) of argv                                   
      52 +  set attachmentList to {}                                     
      53 +  if (count of argv) > (3 + recipientCount) then set attachment
         +List to items (4 + recipientCount) thru (count of argv) of argv
      54    tell application "Mail"
      55      set newMessage to make new outgoing message with properties
           {subject:theSubject, content:theBody, visible:false}
      56      tell newMessage
      57        repeat with addr in recipientList
      58          make new to recipient at end of to recipients with prop
          erties {address:addr}
      59        end repeat
      60 +      repeat with attPath in attachmentList                    
      61 +        make new attachment with properties {file name:(POSIX f
         +ile attPath)} at after the last paragraph                      
      62 +      end repeat                                               
      63        send
      64      end tell
      65    end tell
      66  end run`

⏺ Done. Summary:

  What landed:
  - send_mail now accepts an optional attachments parameter (one or more file
  paths), validated at stage time the same way mkdir validates its target — each
  path must exist and not be a directory, so a typo'd path fails loudly before
  showing a preview rather than mid-osascript call.
  - The preview is restyled to match your example almost exactly:
  The following email will be sent to alice@example.com:

  Subject: ride from airport
  Body:
  Can you pick me up at 5:00pm from the airport? I'm flying Delta.
  Thanks!
  -Jerry

  This cannot be undone once sent — there is no "unsend." Send this email?
  - (plus an Attachments: filename.pdf line when any are present).
  - The stretch goal needs no pipeline work at all — pipeline structurally can't
  include send_mail (mutators are permanently excluded, nothing to roll back),
  so "find my tax return and email it" was already achievable the moment
  attachments existed: two ordinary sequential tool calls, find then send_mail
  with the resolved path.

  One honest caveat, called out prominently in the design note and commit: I
  implemented the osascript attachment syntax (make new attachment with 
  properties {file name:(POSIX file attPath)} at after the last paragraph)
  against the standard documented idiom, but did not verify it against a real
  send — that would mean actually automating your real Mail.app. You should do
  the first live test with an attachment yourself; if the syntax is wrong, it'll
  fail loudly with a real AppleScript error (not silently misbehave).

  Build/vet/gofmt/test all green, including the new attachment-path validation
  tests and an eval case (still safely stage-only, never executes).

✻ Crunched for 4m 57s

※ recap: Building a macOS MCP server with read/write capabilities; just added email attachments support to send_mail. Next: you should send a real test email with an attachment to verify the AppleScript syntax actually works.
